 succeeded in 269ms:
  500: For every fixed real \(s<2\),
  501: \begin{equation}
  502:  a_d(s):=
  503:  \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}
  504:  \asymp_s \exp\{d\mathfrak P(s/2)\}.
  505:  \label{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}
  506: \end{equation}
  507: For every fixed \(s\ge2\), one has instead
  508: \[
  509:  \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d\log a_d(s)=0.
  510: \]
  511: \end{lemma}
  512: 
  513: \begin{proof}
  514: First suppose \(0<s<2\), and put \(\beta=s/2\).  The even Knauf
  515: partition function \(Z^{K,e}_d(2\beta)\) is exactly \(a_d(s)\): its
  516: states are the new Stern--Brocot denominators at depth \(d\).  In the
  517: notation of Fiala, Kleban, and \"Ozl\"uk, the first-coordinate identity
  518: for their Knauf vector, its Perron--Frobenius iteration, and its spectral
  519: asymptotic are given in
  520: \cite[Sec.~IV, especially Eqs.~(23), (26), and (28)]{FialaKlebanOzluk2003}.
  521: They give
  522: \[
  523:  a_d(s)=c_s\lambda(s/2)^d(1+o(1)),\qquad c_s>0.
  524: \]
  525: Their free-energy identification, together with the denominator formula
  526: for Stern--Brocot pressure
  527: \cite[Thm.~1.1 and the denominator formula in Sec.~2]{KessebohmerStratmann2007},
  528: gives \(\log\lambda(s/2)=\mathfrak P(s/2)\).  This proves
  529: \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} in the range \(0<s<2\).  At \(s=0\),
  530: there are exactly \(2^{d-1}\) new denominators and
  531: \(\mathfrak P(0)=\log2\), so the assertion is immediate.
  532: 
  533: It remains to supply the estimate for \(s<0\), a range not covered by
  534: the positive-temperature Perron--Frobenius statement just cited.  Put
  535: \(t=-s>0\), and let
  536: \[
  537:  L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
  538:  R=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
  539:  \lVert M\rVert_\Sigma=\sum_{i,j=1}^2M_{ij}.
  540: \]
  541:  By \Cref{lem:continued-fraction-knauf-normalization}, the new denominators at
  542:  depth \(d\) are in bijection with words \(w\in\{L,R\}^{d-1}\), and
  543: \begin{equation}
  544:  q(w)=\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma.
  545:  \label{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator}
  546: \end{equation}
  547: Define
  548: \[
  549:  Z_n(t):=\sum_{w\in\{L,R\}^n}\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma^t,
  550:  \qquad n\ge0.
  551: \]
  552: For nonnegative matrices \(A,B\),
  553: \(\lVert AB\rVert_\Sigma\le
  554: \lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\), and hence
  555: \begin{equation}
  556:  Z_{m+n}(t)\le Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
  557:  \label{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}
  558: \end{equation}
  559: For the reverse inequality with one bridging letter, write
  560: \(\alpha=A^T(1,1)^T\) and \(\beta=B(1,1)^T\).  Direct multiplication gives
  561: \[
  562:  \lVert ALB\rVert_\Sigma+\lVert ARB\rVert_\Sigma
  563:  =\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma
  564:    +\alpha_1\beta_1+\alpha_2\beta_2.
  565: \]
  566: Thus at least one \(C\in\{L,R\}\) satisfies
  567: \(\lVert ACB\rVert_\Sigma\ge
  568: \frac12\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\).  Summing over
  569: all pairs of words yields
  570: \begin{equation}
  571:  Z_{m+n+1}(t)\ge2^{-t}Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
  572:  \label{eq:matrix-partition-bridged}
  573: \end{equation}
  574: 
  575: Let \(\lambda_t=\lim_n Z_n(t)^{1/n}\), whose existence follows from
  576: \eqref{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}.  That inequality gives
  577: \(Z_n(t)\ge\lambda_t^n\).  Iterating
  578: \eqref{eq:matrix-partition-bridged} \(k-1\) times and then letting
  579: \(k\to\infty\) gives
  580: \[
  581:  Z_{k(n+1)-1}(t)\ge2^{-t(k-1)}Z_n(t)^k,
  582:  \qquad
  583:  Z_n(t)\le2^t\lambda_t^{n+1}.
  584: \]
  585: Consequently \(Z_n(t)\asymp_t\lambda_t^n\).  By
  586: \eqref{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator} and the denominator form of
  587: Stern--Brocot pressure cited above,
  588: \(\log\lambda_t=\mathfrak P(-t/2)\).  Since
  589: \(a_d(-t)=Z_{d-1}(t)\), this proves
  590: \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} for \(s<0\).
  591: 
  592: Finally, the logarithmic assertion for \(s\ge2\) is precisely the
  593: denominator formulation of the zero-pressure part of
  594: \cite[Thm.~1.1]{KessebohmerStratmann2007}.
  595: \end{proof}
  596: 
  597: The following pressure statement is retained because its renewal
  598: normalization is used below.  Its existence, implicit branch, frozen phase,
  599: and nondifferentiable critical point recover the golden-ratio
  600: Bernoulli-convolution results of Feng--Olivier and Feng
  601: \cite{FengOlivier2003,Feng2005}; they are not claimed as new.
  602: 
  603: \begin{theorem}[Pressure in renewal coordinates]
  604: \label{thm:exact-negative-pressure}
  605: \label{thm:all-real-pressure}
  606: For every \(s\in\mathbb R\), the radius of convergence of \(B_s\) is
  607: \begin{equation}
  608:  R_B(s)=\exp\{-\mathfrak P(s/2)/2\}.
  609:  \label{eq:Bs-radius}
  610: \end{equation}
  611: Thus \(R_B(s)<1\) for \(s<2\), and \(R_B(s)=1\) for \(s\ge2\).
  612: The nonnegative real roots of \(B_s(r)=1\) are classified as follows:
  613: \[
  614: \begin{array}{c|c}
  615: s<\sigma_0 & \text{a unique root }r_s\in(0,R_B(s)),\\
  616: s=\sigma_0 & \text{the unique root }r_s=1=R_B(s),\\
  617: s>\sigma_0 & \text{no root in }[0,R_B(s)].
  618: \end{array}
  619: \]
  620: The pressure exists for every real tilt and satisfies
  621: \begin{equation}
  622:  P(-s):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_{-s}(m)
  623:  =\begin{cases}
  624:    -\log r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\
  625:    0,&s\ge\sigma_0.
  626:   \end{cases}
  627:  \label{eq:all-real-pressure}
  628: \end{equation}
  629: Equivalently, for \(t>-\sigma_0\), \(P(t)>0\) is the unique solution of
  630: \begin{equation}
  631:  \sum_{q=2}^{\infty}
  632:  \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  633:  q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1,
  634:  \label{eq:exact-negative-pressure}
  635: \end{equation}
  636: whereas \(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  In particular,
  637: \begin{equation}
  638:  \{t\in\mathbb R:P(t)>0\}=(-\sigma_0,\infty).
  639:  \label{eq:exact-zero-pressure-set}
  640: \end{equation}
  641: 
  642: The restriction of \(P\) to \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\) is real analytic and
  643: strictly convex.  At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), it has the finite one-sided
  644: derivatives
  645: \begin{equation}
  646:  P'_-(t_c)=0,
  647:  \qquad
  648:  P'_+(t_c)=
  649:  \frac{\displaystyle
  650:   \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  651:        q^{-\sigma_0}\log q}
  652:  {\displaystyle
  653:   \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
  654:        c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}}>0.
  655:  \label{eq:critical-pressure-slopes}
  656: \end{equation}
  657: Consequently, \(P\) is not differentiable at the freezing point.
  658: \end{theorem}
  659: 
  660: \begin{proof}
  661: Write \(a_d(s):=b_{2d+1}(s)\).  The negative-continued-fraction
  662: parameterization gives
  663: \[
  664:  a_d(s)=\sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}.
  665: \]
  666: There are \(2^{d-1}\) fractions in this layer, and their denominators satisfy
  667: \(d+1\le q\le F_{d+2}\).  In particular, every \(B_s\) has positive finite
  668: radius.  The layer is precisely the new-denominator layer of the Knauf model.
  669:  The precise estimate in \Cref{lem:knauf-layer-estimate} gives, for fixed
  670: \(s<2\), \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}, and gives
  671: \(d^{-1}\log a_d(s)\to0\) for \(s\ge2\).  Cauchy--Hadamard now proves
  672: \eqref{eq:Bs-radius}: explicitly,
  673: \(B_s(z)=\sum_{d\ge1}a_d(s)z^{2d+1}\), so the cost \(2d+1\) contributes the
  674: factor \(1/2\) in the radius exponent.  Moreover,
  675: \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} shows that
  676: the terms of \(B_s\) do not tend to zero on \(|z|=R_B(s)\) when \(s<2\).
  677: At \(s=2\), the positive real boundary also diverges.  For \(s>2\), Tonelli's
  678: theorem gives
  679: \begin{equation}
  680:  B_s(1)=\sum_{q\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}{q^s}
  681:  =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1<\infty.
  682:  \label{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}
  683: \end{equation}
  684: 
  685: The coefficients are nonnegative and the support contains both \(3\) and
  686: \(5\), so \(B_s\) is strictly increasing on its positive interval of
  687: convergence.  If \(s\le2\), its positive boundary value is infinite; if
  688: \(2<s<\sigma_0\), \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} is greater than one.
  689: These facts give the unique interior root for \(s<\sigma_0\).  At
  690: \(s=\sigma_0\), the boundary value is one, while it is less than one for
  691: \(s>\sigma_0\).  This proves the asserted real-root classification.  The
  692: triangle inequality also shows that, for \(s<\sigma_0\), the only zero of
  693: \(1-B_s(z)\) in \(|z|\le r_s\) is the simple zero \(r_s\): equality forces
  694: the degree-three and degree-five terms to have the same positive phase, and
  695: \(\gcd(3,5)=1\).
  696: 
  697: Concatenation of generating words gives
  698: \(u_{m+n}(s)\ge u_m(s)u_n(s)\).  The letters of costs \(3\) and \(5\) imply
  699: \(u_j(s)>0\) for every \(j\ge8\).  The shifted Fekete argument used in the
  700: proof of the strip result therefore applies without any restriction on
  701: \(s\), and yields
  702: \[
  703:  \gamma_s:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log u_j(s).
  704: \]
  705: If \(s<\sigma_0\), the formal identity
  706: \(U_s=(1-B_s)^{-1}\), positivity, and the preceding root classification show
  707: that the radius of \(U_s\) is \(r_s\).  At \(s=\sigma_0\), it is one.  If
  708: \(s>\sigma_0\), then \(U_s(1)=(1-B_s(1))^{-1}<\infty\), while
  709: \(u_j(s)\ge b_j(s)\) prevents the radius of \(U_s\) from exceeding
  710: \(R_B(s)=1\).  Hence
  711: \[
  712:  R_U(s)=\begin{cases}r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\1,&s\ge\sigma_0,
  713:  \end{cases}
  714:  \qquad \gamma_s=-\log R_U(s)\ge0.
  715: \]
  716: For \(H_m(s)=\sum_{j=0}^m u_j(s)\), the exact renewal identity gives
  717: \[
  718:  H_m(s)\le S_{-s}(m)\le4H_{m+1}(s).
  719: \]
  720: Since \(m^{-1}\log H_m(s)\to\gamma_s\), this proves
  721: \eqref{eq:all-real-pressure} and the root equation.
  722: 
  723: For \(t>-\sigma_0\), put
  724: \[
  725:  \mu_t(p/q):=q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}.
  726: \]
  727: The root equation makes this a probability measure.  The root lies strictly
  728: inside the relevant convergence circle, and
  729: \(\log q\ll d(p/q)\), so the defining series and all its parameter
  730: derivatives converge locally uniformly.  The analytic implicit-function
  731: theorem applies.  With \(X=\log q\) and \(C=c(p/q)\), differentiation gives
  732: \begin{equation}
  733:  P'(t)=\frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}X}{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C},
  734:  \qquad
  735:  P''(t)=
  736:  \frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}[(X-P'(t)C)^2]}
  737:       {\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C}.
  738:  \label{eq:pressure-derivatives}
  739: \end{equation}
  740: The fractions \(1/2\) and \(1/3\) have \((C,q)=(3,2)\) and \((5,3)\),
  741: and \(\log2/3\ne\log3/5\).  Thus the variance in
  742: \eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} is strictly positive.
  743: 
  744: It remains to justify passage to the critical endpoint.  For the canonical
  745: regular continued fraction \(p/q=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]\), with \(a_r>1\),
  746: the conversion between regular and negative continued fractions gives
  747: \[
  748:  d(p/q)=a_1+\cdots+a_r-1,
  749:  \qquad c(p/q)=2(a_1+\cdots+a_r)-1.
  750: \]
  751: The fixed-denominator mean theorem of Panov and Liehl
  752: \cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} states that
  753: \[
  754:  \frac1{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}
  755:  \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}(a_1+\cdots+a_r)
  756:  \sim\frac6{\pi^2}(\log q)^2.
  757: \]
  758: Consequently, both the numerator and denominator in
  759: \eqref{eq:critical-pressure-slopes} converge absolutely, since they are
  760: bounded by constant multiples of

 succeeded in 284ms:
  760: bounded by constant multiples of
  761: \(\sum q^{1-\sigma_0}\log q\) and
  762: \(\sum q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2\), respectively.  Dominated convergence in
  763: the first identity of \eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} gives the stated finite
  764: positive right derivative.  The pressure is identically zero to the left of
  765: \(t_c\), so its left derivative is zero.
  766: \end{proof}
  767: 
  768: \begin{theorem}[Full large deviations across the freezing corner]
  769: \label{thm:full-fiber-ldp}
  770: Let \(\mathbf X_m\) be uniformly distributed on \(X_m\), and set
  771: \[
  772:  \begin{gathered}
  773:  y_m(x):=\frac1m\log d_m(x),\qquad
  774:  Y_m:=y_m(\mathbf X_m),\\
  775:  t_c:=-\sigma_0,\qquad v_c:=P'_+(t_c),
  776:  \qquad \alpha_+:=\frac12\log\varphi.
  777:  \end{gathered}
  778: \]
  779: Then \((Y_m)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle with speed \(m\)
  780: and good rate function
  781: \begin{equation}
  782:  I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb R}
  783:  \{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
  784:  \label{eq:full-fiber-ldp-rate}
  785: \end{equation}
  786: Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\).  On the coexistence interval,
  787: \begin{equation}
  788:  I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
  789:  \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
  790:  \label{eq:frozen-affine-rate}
  791: \end{equation}
  792: More precisely, for every \(0<\alpha<v_c\),
  793: \begin{equation}
  794:  \begin{aligned}
  795:  &\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
  796:  \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}\\
  797:  &\quad=
  798:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\limsup_{m\to\infty}
  799:  \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}\\
  800:  &\quad=-\bigl(\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha\bigr).
  801:  \end{aligned}
  802:  \label{eq:frozen-local-large-deviation}
  803: \end{equation}
  804: \end{theorem}
  805: 
  806: \begin{proof}
  807: For \(\alpha\in\mathbb R\) and \(\varepsilon>0\), write
  808: \[
  809:  A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  810:  :=\card{\left\{x\in X_m:
  811:  \left|m^{-1}\log d_m(x)-\alpha\right|<\varepsilon\right\}}.
  812: \]
  813: We first establish the lower bound at the nonexposed slopes.  Fix
  814: \(t>t_c\), put \(v=P'(t)>v_c\), and define the tilted law on \(X_n\) by
  815: \[
  816:  \nu_{n,t}(x):=\frac{d_n(x)^t}{S_t(n)}.
  817: \]
  818: For sufficiently small \(s>0\), the exponential Markov inequality and
  819: \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} give
  820: \[
  821:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n
  822:  \log\nu_{n,t}\{y_n\ge v+\delta\}
  823:  \le P(t+s)-P(t)-s(v+\delta)<0.
  824: \]
  825: The corresponding estimate with \(s<0\) gives the same conclusion for
  826: \(y_n\le v-\delta\).  Consequently,
  827: \[
  828:  \sum_{\substack{x\in X_n\\|y_n(x)-v|<\delta}}d_n(x)^t
  829:  =\exp\{n(P(t)+o(1))\}.
  830: \]
  831: Every summand on the left is at most
  832: \(\exp\{n(tv+|t|\delta)\}\), and hence
  833: \begin{equation}
  834:  \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log A_n(v,\delta)
  835:  \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
  836:  \label{eq:exposed-source-count}
  837: \end{equation}
  838: 
  839: We now transfer these source states to a larger layer without changing their
  840: multiplicities.  The proof of \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} gives the
  841: explicit bijection
  842: \[
  843:  \chi_n(x)=
  844:  \begin{cases}
  845:   F_{n+2}+V_n(x),&0\le V_n(x)\le F_{n+1}-2,\\
  846:   V_n(x),&F_{n+1}-1\le V_n(x)<F_{n+2},
  847:  \end{cases}
  848: \]
  849: from \(X_n\) onto the integer points of
  850: \(\mathcal I_n\sqcup\mathcal I_{n+1}\), with
  851: \(d_n(x)=R(\chi_n(x))\).  Take
  852: \(\delta<v/2\), so their multiplicities exceed two for all sufficiently
  853: large \(n\).  The uniform layer-count lemma shows that each Weinstein
  854: generating orbit supplies at most four source states to these two layers.
  855: The specialized formulas in the proof of
  856: \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} give
  857: \[
  858:  L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,\qquad
  859:  L([a]\tau g)=L(g)+1+2a,
  860: \]
  861: and the two sigma branches increase these lengths by one.  Hence every orbit
  862: meeting \(\mathcal I_n\sqcup\mathcal I_{n+1}\) has generator length at most
  863: \(n+1\).
  864: Keeping the generating number of every orbit which occurs therefore gives a
  865: set \(\mathcal G_n\) such that
  866: \begin{equation}
  867:  \card{\mathcal G_n}\ge\frac14 A_n(v,\delta),
  868:  \qquad L(g)\le n+1\quad(g\in\mathcal G_n),
  869:  \label{eq:source-generating-orbits}
  870: \end{equation}
  871: and \(n^{-1}\log R(g)\in(v-\delta,v+\delta)\), since the orbit action
  872: preserves \(R\).  Distinct elements of
  873: \(\mathcal G_n\) generate disjoint orbits.
  874: 
  875: Fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\), set \(\rho=\alpha/v\), and let
  876: \(n_m=\lfloor\rho m\rfloor\).  For sufficiently large \(m\), every
  877: \(g\in\mathcal G_{n_m}\) has \(L(g)<m\).  The generator formulas in the
  878: proof of \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} permit the parity-free
  879: definition
  880: \[
  881:  z_m(g):=
  882:  \begin{cases}
  883:  [a]g,&m-L(g)=2a,\\
  884:  [a]\tau g,&m-L(g)=2a+1.
  885:  \end{cases}
  886: \]
  887: They give \(L(z_m(g))=m\) and \(R(z_m(g))=R(g)>1\).  Thus
  888: \(z_m(g)\in\mathcal I_m\), and \(\chi_m^{-1}\) turns these into distinct
  889: states of \(X_m\).  Moreover,
  890: \[
  891:  \left|m^{-1}\log R(z_m(g))-\alpha\right|
  892:  \le \frac{n_m}{m}\delta+\frac vm.
  893: \]
  894: Combining this injection with
  895: \eqref{eq:exposed-source-count}--\eqref{eq:source-generating-orbits} yields,
  896: for every \(\varepsilon>\rho\delta\),
  897: \begin{equation}
  898:  \liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  899:  \ge \rho\bigl(P(t)-tv-|t|\delta\bigr).
  900:  \label{eq:orbit-filling-lower-bound}
  901: \end{equation}
  902: First let \(\delta\downarrow0\), and then let \(t\downarrow t_c\).  Since
  903: \(P(t)\to0\), \(P'(t)=v\to v_c\), and
  904: \[
  905:  \rho(P(t)-tv)=\alpha\left(\frac{P(t)}v-t\right)
  906:  \longrightarrow-t_c\alpha=\sigma_0\alpha,
  907: \]
  908: we obtain
  909: \begin{equation}
  910:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
  911:  \frac1m\log A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  912:  \ge\sigma_0\alpha.
  913:  \label{eq:frozen-count-lower}
  914: \end{equation}
  915: 
  916: The matching upper bound uses the critical pressure directly.  On the set
  917: counted by \(A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)\),
  918: \(d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}\ge
  919: \exp\{-\sigma_0m(\alpha+\varepsilon)\}\).  Therefore
  920: \[
  921:  A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
  922:  e^{-\sigma_0m(\alpha+\varepsilon)}
  923:  \le S_{-\sigma_0}(m)=e^{o(m)},
  924: \]
  925: which, together with \eqref{eq:frozen-count-lower}, proves the corresponding
  926: counting limit.  Since \(\card{X_m}=F_{m+2}\) and
  927: \(m^{-1}\log F_{m+2}\to\log\varphi\), this is
  928: \eqref{eq:frozen-local-large-deviation}.
  929: 
  930: We next identify the convex dual.  If \(0\le\alpha\le v_c\), then
  931: \(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le t_c\), while convexity and the right derivative at
  932: \(t_c\) give \(P(t)\ge v_c(t-t_c)\) for \(t\ge t_c\).  It follows that
  933: \(t\alpha-P(t)\le t_c\alpha\) for every \(t\), with equality at
  934: \(t=t_c\), proving \eqref{eq:frozen-affine-rate}.
  935: 
  936: The exact maximum theorem gives
  937: \[
  938:  \max_{x\in X_m}m^{-1}\log d_m(x)\longrightarrow\alpha_+.
  939: \]
  940: For \(t>0\), the bounds
  941: \(D_m^t\le S_t(m)\le F_{m+2}D_m^t\) imply
  942: \[
  943:  \alpha_+\le\frac{P(t)}t
  944:  \le\alpha_++\frac{\log\varphi}{t}.
  945: \]
  946: By convexity, (P') is increasing and therefore has a limit in
  947: \((0,+\infty]\).  The identity
  948: \[
  949:  \frac{P(t)}t=\frac{P(0)}t+\frac1t\int_0^t P'(u)\,du
  950: \]
  951: and Ces\`aro convergence show that this limit is \(\alpha_+\).  Thus
  952: \(P'(t)\uparrow\alpha_+\) as \(t\to\infty\).  Strict convexity and
  953: analyticity on \((t_c,\infty)\) now show that every
  954: \(\alpha\in(v_c,\alpha_+)\) is exposed by a unique \(t>t_c\).  The tilted
  955: estimate above with \(n=m\) supplies
  956: \begin{equation}
  957:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
  958:  \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}
  959:  \ge P(t)-t\alpha-\log\varphi=-I(\alpha).
  960:  \label{eq:exposed-local-large-deviation}
  961: \end{equation}
  962: 
  963: Finally, the logarithmic moment generating functions under the uniform law
  964: converge on all of \(\mathbb R\) to
  965: \[
  966:  \Lambda(t)=P(t)-\log\varphi.
  967: \]
  968: The variables are eventually supported in a fixed compact interval, so they
  969: are exponentially tight.  The G\"artner--Ellis upper bound
  970: \cite[Theorem~2.3.6(a)]{DemboZeitouni2010} therefore gives the
  971: large-deviation upper bound for all closed sets.
  972: 
  973: It remains to justify the effective domain and endpoint lower bounds.  If
  974: \(\alpha<0\), letting \(t\to-\infty\) in
  975: \eqref{eq:full-fiber-ldp-rate} and using \(P(t)=0\) gives
  976: \(I(\alpha)=+\infty\).  If \(\alpha>\alpha_+\), the limit
  977: \(P(t)/t\to\alpha_+\) gives the same conclusion as \(t\to\infty\).
  978: Conversely, if \(0\le\alpha\le\alpha_+\), then
  979: \(t\alpha-P(t)\) is bounded above on \(( -\infty,t_c]\), on the compact
  980: interval \([t_c,0]\), and on \([0,\infty)\), where
  981: \(P(t)\ge t\alpha_+\).  Thus the effective domain is exactly
  982: \([0,\alpha_+]\).  The rate is a lower-semicontinuous convex function, and
  983: its finiteness on this closed interval implies continuity relative to the
  984: interval, including at the endpoints.
  985: 
  986: The local lower bounds already proved cover the coexistence and exposed
  987: parts of \((0,\alpha_+)\), and points from either side approximate \(v_c\).
  988: At \(0\), the two singleton fibers give the lower bound directly.  At
  989: \(\alpha_+\), continuity of \(I\) and exposed points approaching from below
  990: supply the lower bound for every open neighborhood.  Hence the lower bound
  991: holds for every open set.  The effective domain is compact and \(I\) is
  992: lower semicontinuous, so \(I\) is good and the full large deviation principle
  993: follows.
  994: \end{proof}
  995: 
  996: \subsection{Microcanonical phase separation}
  997: 
  998: The renewal description also retains the generating layer, rather than only
  999: the multiplicity.  Let
 1000: \[
 1001:  \mathcal A:=\{p/q:0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\},
 1002:  \qquad \pi(p/q):=q^{-\sigma _0}.
 1003: \]
 1004: The critical identity \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} gives
 1005: \(\sum_{\mathcal A}\pi=1\).  Let \((C_i,H_i)_{i\ge1}\) be independent with
 1006: \[
 1007:  \Pr\{(C_i,H_i)=(c(p/q),\log q)\}=\pi(p/q),
 1008: \]
 1009: put \(S_n=\sum_{i=1}^nC_i\), \(T_n=\sum_{i=1}^nH_i\), and define the marked
 1010: renewal measure
 1011: \[
 1012:  \mathcal U:=\sum_{n\ge1}\mathcal L(S_n,T_n).
 1013: \]
 1014: The moment estimates in the proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} show that
 1015: \[
 1016:  \mu_C:=\mathbb E C_1<\infty,
 1017:  \qquad \mu_H:=\mathbb E H_1<\infty,
 1018:  \qquad \frac{\mu_H}{\mu_C}=v_c.
 1019: \]
 1020: 
 1021: We shall use the following marked form of the already proved weighted renewal
 1022: identity.  It is recorded explicitly to retain the generating cost in the
 1023: conditional argument.  For $x\in X_m$ with \(d_m(x)>2\), let \(g_m(x)\) be
 1024: the unique generating number of the Weinstein orbit containing \(\chi_m(x)\).
 1025: Define
 1026: \[
 1027:  K_m(c):=
 1028:  \begin{cases}
 1029:   4,&c\le m-1,\\
 1030:   3,&c=m,\\
 1031:   1,&c=m+1,\\
 1032:   0,&c\ge m+2.
 1033:  \end{cases}
 1034: \]
 1035: For every Borel set \(B\subset\mathbb R\times(\log2,\infty)\),
 1036: \begin{equation}
 1037:  \card{\{x\in X_m:(L(g_m(x)),\log d_m(x))\in B\}}
 1038:  =\int_B K_m(s)e^{\sigma_0t}\,\mathcal U(ds,dt).
 1039:  \label{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}
 1040: \end{equation}
 1041: Indeed, for a generating number \(g\) of cost \(c=L(g)\), the four branches
 1042: and their layer indices are
 1043: \[
 1044:  \begin{array}{c|c}
 1045:  {}[a]g&c+2a\\
 1046:  {}[a]\tau g&c+1+2a\\
 1047:  \sigma([a]g)&c+1+2a\\
 1048:  \sigma([a]\tau g)&c+2+2a.
 1049:  \end{array}
 1050: \]
 1051: The free action makes the middle two points distinct.  Thus this orbit
 1052: contributes zero, one, or two points to \(\mathcal I_j\), according as
 1053: \(j<c\), \(j=c\), or \(j>c\).  Summing over the two disjoint layers
 1054: \(\mathcal I_m\sqcup\mathcal I_{m+1}\) gives exactly \(K_m(c)\).  The two
 1055: unit-multiplicity endpoints correspond to the empty word and are excluded by
 1056: the condition \(t>\log2\).
 1057: 
 1058: Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a nonempty word
 1059: \(w=(p_1/q_1)\times\cdots\times(p_n/q_n)\) has
 1060: \[
 1061:  L(g)=S_n,\qquad \log R(g)=T_n,
 1062:  \qquad \pi(w)=e^{-\sigma_0T_n}.
 1063: \]
 1064: Multiplication of its renewal mass by \(e^{\sigma_0T_n}\), followed by the
 1065: orbit count \(K_m(S_n)\), therefore counts every corresponding finite-window
 1066: state once.  This proves \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}.
 1067: 
 1068: For the endpoint tilt, define the finite-window Gibbs measure
 1069: \[
 1070:  \widehat Q_m(x):=\frac{d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}}{S_{-\sigma_0}(m)}
 1071:  \qquad(x\in X_m),
 1072: \]
 1073: and put \(\ell_m(x)=L(g_m(x))\) when \(d_m(x)>2\), while
 1074: \(\ell_m(x)=0\) when \(d_m(x)\le2\).
 1075: 
 1076: \begin{theorem}[Critical finite-size and coexistence law]
 1077: \label{thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs}
 1078: Let
 1079: \[
 1080:  \mu_C=
 1081:  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 1082:  c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}.
 1083: \]
 1084: Then
 1085: \begin{equation}
 1086:  S_{-\sigma_0}(m)\sim\frac{4m}{\mu_C}.
 1087:  \label{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}
 1088: \end{equation}
 1089: If \(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m\) has law \(\widehat Q_m\) and
 1090: \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\), then
 1091: \begin{equation}
 1092:  \left(
 1093:   \frac1m\log d_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m),
 1094:   \frac1m\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 1095:  \right)
 1096:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\mathrm d}(v_cU,U).
 1097:  \label{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}
 1098: \end{equation}
 1099: \end{theorem}
 1100: 
 1101: \begin{proof}
 1102: At \(s=\sigma_0\), the coefficients \(b_j(\sigma_0)\) form a probability
 1103: distribution on the positive integers by
 1104: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}.  Its span is one because its support
 1105: contains both \(3\) and \(5\), and its mean is \(\mu_C<\infty\).  The
 1106: arithmetic renewal theorem therefore gives
 1107: \[
 1108:  u_j(\sigma_0)\longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}.
 1109: \]
 1110: Substitution in the exact identity
 1111: \eqref{eq:weighted-window-renewal}, followed by Ces\`aro summation, proves
 1112: \eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}.
 1113: 
 1114: We use the critical letter law \(\pi(p/q)=q^{-\sigma_0}\) and the marked
 1115: renewal variables \((S_n,T_n)\) defined above
 1116: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}.  For every bounded continuous
 1117: function \(f\) on \([0,1]\), the arithmetic renewal limit and a Riemann-sum
 1118: argument give
 1119: \[
 1120:  \frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)f(j/m)
 1121:  \longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}\int_0^1 f(y)\,dy.
 1122: \]
 1123: The terms at \(j=m,m+1\) are bounded, while
 1124: \(K_m(j)=4\) for \(j\le m-1\).  The total critical weight of the states
 1125: with multiplicity at most two is also bounded uniformly in \(m\).
 1126: Multiplying \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} by
 1127: \(e^{-\sigma_0t}\), and then using
 1128: \eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}, proves
 1129: \begin{equation}
 1130:  \frac{\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)}m
 1131:  \xrightarrow{\mathrm d}U.
 1132:  \label{eq:critical-uniform-layer}
 1133: \end{equation}
 1134: 
 1135: It remains to retain the reward mark.  The strong law gives
 1136: \(T_n/S_n\to\mu_H/\mu_C=v_c\) almost surely.  For \(\delta>0\), let
 1137: \[
 1138:  A_m=\mathcal U\{0<S\le m+1,\ |T/S-v_c|>\delta\}.
 1139: \]
 1140: Every renewal step has cost at least three.  Hence, on each sample path, the

 succeeded in 370ms:

  362: Consequently, for every \(m\ge1\),
  363: \begin{equation}
  364:  S_{-s}(m)
  365:  =4\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(s)+3u_m(s)+u_{m+1}(s)-2.
  366:  \label{eq:weighted-window-renewal}
  367: \end{equation}
  368: Moreover, with formal power series
  369: \[
  370:  B_s(z):=\sum_{\ell\ge1}b_\ell(s)z^\ell,
  371:  \qquad U_s(z):=\sum_{j\ge0}u_j(s)z^j,
  372: \]
  373: one has
  374: \begin{equation}
  375:  U_s(z)=\frac1{1-B_s(z)}.
  376:  \label{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function}
  377: \end{equation}
  378: \end{proposition}
  379: 
  380: \begin{proof}
  381: We use the generating-number parameterization in
  382: \cite[Secs.~3--4]{Weinstein2016}, but not its layer-stabilization theorem.
  383: Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a generating number \(g\) corresponds
  384: uniquely to a nonempty word
  385: \[
  386:  w=\frac{p_1}{q_1}\times\cdots\times\frac{p_r}{q_r}
  387: \]
  388: in reduced fractions from \((0,1)\).  Proposition~3.3 and formula~(16) of
  389: that paper give, in the present notation,
  390: \begin{equation}
  391:  R(g)=\prod_{i=1}^r q_i,
  392:  \qquad L(g)=\sum_{i=1}^r c(p_i/q_i).
  393:  \label{eq:generator-weight-length}
  394: \end{equation}
  395: The continued-fraction bijection also gives
  396: \eqref{eq:weighted-letter-coefficients}.  Unique decomposition of a free word
  397: therefore shows that \(u_j(s)\) is exactly the sum of \(R(g)^{-s}\) over all
  398: generating numbers with \(L(g)=j\), and proves
  399: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} as a formal identity.
  400: 
  401: It remains to count each generating orbit in a fixed layer.  A generating
  402: number belongs to Weinstein's odd first-index sheet, so its initial-block
  403: parameter is \(1\).  The formulas already established in
  404: \eqref{eq:L-a-shift}--\eqref{eq:L-sigma-shift} consequently specialize to
  405: \[
  406:  L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,\qquad
  407:  L([a]\tau g)=L(g)+1+2a,\qquad
  408:  L(\sigma n)=L(n)+1
  409: \]
  410: for \(a\ge0\).  Hence the two branches in Weinstein's first sheet contribute
  411: one point to every layer \(j\ge L(g)\); their sigma images contribute one
  412: further point precisely when \(j>L(g)\).  The action is free and distinct
  413: generating orbits are disjoint.  Since \(\mathcal I_j\) contains exactly one
  414: point of partition multiplicity \(1\) for \(j\ge1\), summing the orbit weights
  415: gives \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}.  The two-layer identity
  416: \eqref{eq:real-collision-two-layers} then gives
  417: \eqref{eq:weighted-window-renewal}.  Notice that the restriction \(j\ge1\) is
  418: essential in \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}, because \(\mathcal I_0\) is
  419: empty.
  420: \end{proof}

  422: Let \(\mathfrak P\) denote the Stern--Brocot pressure
  423: \[
  424:  \mathfrak P(\theta)
  425:  :=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d
  426:    \log\sum_{T\in\mathcal T_d}|T|^\theta .
  427: \]
  428: We use the generation convention in which the first interior fraction
  429: \(1/2\) has depth \(d=1\).  The denominator form of the same pressure
  430: \cite[Sec.~2 and Thm.~1.1]{KessebohmerStratmann2007} is
  431: \begin{equation}
  432:  \mathfrak P(\theta)=
  433:  \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d\log
  434:  \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-2\theta}.
  435:  \label{eq:stern-brocot-denominator-convention}
  436: \end{equation}
  437: 
  438: \begin{lemma}[Continued-fraction/Knauf normalization]
  439: \label{lem:continued-fraction-knauf-normalization}
  440: For \(d\ge1\), write a composition of \(d\) as
  441: \(d=e_1+\cdots+e_r\), and put \(a_i=e_i+1\).  Encode the composition by the
  442: word \(w=w_1\cdots w_{d-1}\in\{L,R\}^{d-1}\) whose \(j\)-th letter is \(R\)
  443: exactly when \(j=e_1+\cdots+e_i\) for some \(i<r\), and is \(L\) otherwise.
  444: With
  445: \[
  446:  L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
  447:  R=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad M_w=w_1\cdots w_{d-1},
  448: \]
  449: the map \((a_1,\ldots,a_r)\mapsto w\) is a bijection, and
  450: \begin{equation}
  451:  \begin{aligned}
  452:  \frac pq&=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]_-=
  453:  \frac{\text{sum of the first row of }M_w}{\|M_w\|_\Sigma},\\
  454:  q&=\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)=\|M_w\|_\Sigma.
  455:  \end{aligned}
  456:  \label{eq:negative-cf-word-bijection}
  457: \end{equation}
  458: Thus \(d(p/q)=d\) is Knauf generation \(d\), the corresponding matrix word
  459: has length \(d-1\), and the generator cost is \(c(p/q)=2d+1\).  In particular,
  460: for \(\beta\in\mathbb R\), the even Knauf sum in the convention of
  461: \cite[Sec.~IV]{FialaKlebanOzluk2003} is exactly
  462: \[
  463:  Z^{K,e}_d(2\beta)
  464:  =\sum_{d(p/q)=d}q^{-2\beta}.
  465: \]
  466: There is no \(d=0\) fraction in \((0,1)\); the first four nonempty layers are
  467: \[
  468: \begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
  469: d&\text{words}&q=\|M_w\|_\Sigma\text{ in word order}&c=2d+1\\ \hline
  470: 1&\varnothing&2&3\\
  471: 2&L,R&3,\,3&5\\
  472: 3&LL,LR,RL,RR&4,\,5,\,5,\,4&7\\
  473: 4&\{L,R\}^3&5,\,7,\,8,\,7,\,7,\,8,\,7,\,5&9
  474: \end{array}
  475: \]
  476: \end{lemma}
  477: 
  478: \begin{proof}
  479: Choosing the separator positions of a composition is the standard bijection
  480: between compositions of \(d\) and binary words of length \(d-1\).  Starting
  481: from \(M_\varnothing=I\), simultaneous induction on the word length, using
  482: right multiplication by \(L\) or \(R\), gives the displayed subtractive
  483: Euclidean recursion for the numerator and the negative-continuant recursion
  484: for the denominator.  Explicitly, appending \(L\) replaces the last
  485: composition part \(e_r\) by \(e_r+1\), whereas appending \(R\) replaces it by
  486: \((e_r,1)\); substituting these two operations in
  487: \(\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)
  488: =a_r\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-1})
  489: -\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-2})\) agrees with
  490: \(M_{wL}=M_wL\) and \(M_{wR}=M_wR\), respectively.  This proves
  491: \eqref{eq:negative-cf-word-bijection}.
  492: The depth and cost identities follow from the definitions.  The Knauf
  493: identity is then term-by-term: its inverse temperature is \(2\beta\), so its
  494: denominator weight is \(q^{-2\beta}\).  The table also fixes the empty-word
  495: and first-generation conventions explicitly.
  496: \end{proof}

  498: \begin{lemma}[New-denominator layer estimate]
  499: \label{lem:knauf-layer-estimate}

  504:  \asymp_s \exp\{d\mathfrak P(s/2)\}.
  505:  \label{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}
  506: \end{equation}
  507: For every fixed \(s\ge2\), one has instead
  508: \[
  509:  \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d\log a_d(s)=0.
  510: \]
  511: \end{lemma}
  512: 
  513: \begin{proof}
  514: First suppose \(0<s<2\), and put \(\beta=s/2\).  The even Knauf
  515: partition function \(Z^{K,e}_d(2\beta)\) is exactly \(a_d(s)\): its

 1140: Every renewal step has cost at least three.  Hence, on each sample path, the
 1141: number of renewal epochs with \(S_n\le m+1\), divided by \(m\), is bounded;
 1142: the corresponding number of exceptional epochs divided by \(m\) tends to
 1143: zero by the strong law.  Dominated convergence yields \(A_m=o(m)\).
 1144: Since \(K_m\le4\), the marked identity and

 1147:  \frac1m\log d_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 1148:  -v_c\frac1m\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 1149:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}0.
 1150: \]
 1151: Together with \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-layer}, this proves
 1152: \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}.
 1153: \end{proof}

 1155: \begin{proposition}[Finite-prime-support coefficient interface]
 1156: \label{prop:prime-support-interface}
 1157: Fix distinct primes \(\mathcal P=\{p_1,\ldots,p_d\}\).  For
 1158: \(\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_d)\in\mathbb N^d\setminus\{\mathbf0\}\), put
 1159: \[
 1160:  q_{\mathbf a}:=\prod_{i=1}^d p_i^{a_i},
 1161:  \qquad
 1162:  A_{\mathbf a}(u):=
 1163:  \sum_{\substack{1\le h<q_{\mathbf a}\\(h,q_{\mathbf a})=1}}
 1164:  u^{c(h/q_{\mathbf a})},
 1165: \]
 1166: and define the formal series
 1167: \[
 1168:  A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,u)
 1169:  :=\sum_{\mathbf a\ne\mathbf0}
 1170:  A_{\mathbf a}(u)\mathbf z^{\mathbf a}.
 1171: \]
 1172: If
 1173: \[
 1174:  U_j(q_{\mathbf n})
 1175:  :=\card{\{g:\text{\(g\) is a Weinstein generator, }
 1176:  R(g)=q_{\mathbf n},\ L(g)=j\}},
 1177: \]
 1178: with the empty-word convention \(U_0(1)=1\), then
 1179: \begin{equation}
 1180:  \sum_{\mathbf n\in\mathbb N^d}\sum_{j\ge0}
 1181:  U_j(q_{\mathbf n})\mathbf z^{\mathbf n}u^j
 1182:  =\frac1{1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,u)}.
 1183:  \label{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal}
 1184: \end{equation}
 1185: For \(q_{\mathbf n}>2\), the exact finite-window count is
 1186: \begin{equation}
 1187:  C_m(q_{\mathbf n})
 1188:  =\sum_{j\ge0}K_m(j)U_j(q_{\mathbf n}).
 1189:  \label{eq:prime-support-window-interface}
 1190: \end{equation}
 1191: After the cost mark is removed, the series is rational:
 1192: \begin{equation}
 1193:  \sum_{\mathbf n\in\mathbb N^d}
 1194:  \left(\sum_{j\ge0}U_j(q_{\mathbf n})\right)\mathbf z^{\mathbf n}
 1195:  =\frac1{\displaystyle
 1196:  2-\prod_{i=1}^d\frac{1-z_i}{1-p_i z_i}}.
 1197:  \label{eq:prime-support-unmarked-rational}
 1198: \end{equation}
 1199: \end{proposition}
 1200: 
 1201: \begin{proof}
 1202: Under Weinstein's free-monoid parameterization, a letter with denominator
 1203: \(q_{\mathbf a}\) contributes exponent vector \(\mathbf a\), cost
 1204: \(c(h/q_{\mathbf a})\), and multiplier \(q_{\mathbf a}\).  Unique ordered
 1205: factorization into letters gives \eqref{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal} by the
 1206: formal sequence construction.  The four orbit branches counted in
 1207: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} depend only on the generator cost and
 1208: give \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface}.  Finally,
 1209: \[
 1210:  \sum_{a\ge0}\phi_{\rm E}(p_i^a)z_i^a
 1211:  =\frac{1-z_i}{1-p_i z_i}.
 1212: \]
 1213: Multiplying over the primes shows that
 1214: \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)\) is the displayed product minus one, which
 1215: proves \eqref{eq:prime-support-unmarked-rational}.
 1216: \end{proof}
 1217: 
 1218: % The former directional quenched theorem is withdrawn: its proof required a
 1219: % parameter-uniform smooth-point expansion and an unbounded-cost truncation
 1220: % estimate that are not established here.
 1221: \iffalse
 1222: Write
 1223: \[
 1224:  \mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}:=
 1225:  \{\boldsymbol\rho\in(0,\infty)^d:
 1226:    \rho_1+\cdots+\rho_d=1\}.
 1227: \]
 1228: 
 1229: \begin{theorem}[Directional quenched law on finite prime support]
 1230: \label{thm:prime-support-directional-quenched}
 1231: Retain the notation of \Cref{prop:prime-support-interface}, and fix
 1232: \(\boldsymbol\rho\in\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\).  Put
 1233: \[
 1234:  g_i(z):=\frac{1-z}{1-p_i z},
 1235:  \qquad
 1236:  h_i(z):=\frac{z g_i'(z)}{g_i(z)}
 1237:  =\frac{(p_i-1)z}{(1-z)(1-p_i z)}.
 1238: \]
 1239: There are unique \(\tau>0\) and
 1240: \(\mathbf z=\mathbf z(\boldsymbol\rho)\in
 1241: \prod_i(0,p_i^{-1})\) such that
 1242: \begin{equation}
 1243:  \prod_{i=1}^d g_i(z_i)=2,
 1244:  \qquad h_i(z_i)=\tau\rho_i\quad(1\le i\le d).
 1245:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-saddle}
 1246: \end{equation}
 1247: More explicitly, \(z_i=\zeta_i(\tau\rho_i)\), where
 1248: \begin{equation}
 1249:  \zeta_i(x)=
 1250:  \frac{(p_i-1)+(p_i+1)x-
 1251:   \sqrt{((p_i-1)+(p_i+1)x)^2-4p_i x^2}}
 1252:  {2p_i x}.
 1253:  \label{eq:prime-support-correct-inverse}
 1254: \end{equation}
 1255: The point \(\mathbf z\) is the unique point of
 1256: \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf w,1)=1\) in the closed polydisc
 1257: \(\{|w_i|\le z_i\}\).
 1258: 
 1259: Give every letter \((\mathbf a,h/q_{\mathbf a})\) the probability
 1260: \begin{equation}
 1261:  \Pr_{\mathbf z}\{(\mathbf A,C)=
 1262:   (\mathbf a,c(h/q_{\mathbf a}))\}=\mathbf z^{\mathbf a}.
 1263:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-letter-law}
 1264: \end{equation}
 1265: This is a probability law, and
 1266: \[
 1267:  \mathbb E_{\mathbf z}\mathbf A=2\tau\boldsymbol\rho,
 1268:  \qquad m_C(\boldsymbol\rho):=\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}C<\infty.
 1269: \]
 1270: Define its directional velocity by
 1271: \begin{equation}
 1272:  v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}:=
 1273:  \frac{2\tau\sum_i\rho_i\log p_i}{m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)}.
 1274:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-velocity}
 1275: \end{equation}
 1276: 
 1277: Let \(\mathbf n_N=N\boldsymbol\rho+O(1)\in\mathbb N^d\), and let
 1278: \(W_N\) be uniform among the nonempty free generator words of total exponent
 1279: \(\mathbf n_N\).  If \(R_N\) is its number of letters and \(T_N\) its
 1280: total cost, then
 1281: \begin{equation}
 1282:  \frac{R_N}{N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac1{2\tau},
 1283:  \qquad
 1284:  \frac{T_N}{N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)}{2\tau},
 1285:  \qquad
 1286:  \frac{\log q_{\mathbf n_N}}{T_N}
 1287:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}.
 1288:  \label{eq:prime-support-directional-free-law}
 1289: \end{equation}
 1290: 
 1291: Finally, let \(m_N\to\infty\) be integers such that
 1292: \begin{equation}
 1293:  \frac{\log q_{\mathbf n_N}}{m_N}\longrightarrow
 1294:  \alpha\in[0,v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}).
 1295:  \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-budget}
 1296: \end{equation}
 1297: Then the exact level
 1298: \(\{x\in X_{m_N}:d_{m_N}(x)=q_{\mathbf n_N}\}\) is nonempty for all
 1299: sufficiently large \(N\),
 1300: \begin{equation}
 1301:  C_{m_N}(q_{\mathbf n_N})
 1302:  \sim4\card{\{\text{free words of exponent }\mathbf n_N\}},
 1303:  \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-count}
 1304: \end{equation}
 1305: and, under the uniform law on this level,
 1306: \begin{equation}
 1307:  \frac{L(g_{m_N})}{m_N}
 1308:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}
 1309:  \frac{\alpha}{v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}}.
 1310:  \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-window-law}
 1311: \end{equation}
 1312: The free-word convergences are uniform when \(\boldsymbol\rho\) ranges over a
 1313: compact subset of \(\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\) and
 1314: \(\|\mathbf n_N-N\boldsymbol\rho\|\) is bounded.  The finite-window
 1315: conclusions are uniform under the additional requirement that the budget
 1316: ratio remain bounded away from one.
 1317: \end{theorem}
 1318: 
 1319: \begin{proof}
 1320: Each \(h_i\) is strictly increasing from zero to infinity on
 1321: \((0,p_i^{-1})\).  Solving the resulting quadratic gives
 1322: \eqref{eq:prime-support-correct-inverse}.  Hence
 1323: \(\prod_i g_i(\zeta_i(\tau\rho_i))\) increases continuously from one to
 1324: infinity, which proves existence and uniqueness in
 1325: \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-saddle}.  Since
 1326: \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)=1\),
 1327: \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-letter-law} has total mass one, and
 1328: Euler differentiation gives
 1329: \[
 1330:  \mathbb E_{\mathbf z}A_i
 1331:  =z_i\partial_{z_i}A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)
 1332:  =2h_i(z_i)=2\tau\rho_i.
 1333: \]
 1334: 
 1335: We verify the strict-minimality and lattice hypotheses needed below.  If
 1336: \(|w_i|\le z_i\) and \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf w,1)=1\), coefficient
 1337: positivity gives
 1338: \[
 1339:  1\le A_{\mathcal P}(|\mathbf w|,1)
 1340:  \le A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)=1.
 1341: \]
 1342: The support contains every \(\mathbf e_i\), so equality in the second
 1343: inequality forces \(|w_i|=z_i\) for every \(i\).  Equality in the triangle
 1344: inequality then forces all supported monomials to have phase one; applying
 1345: this to the \(\mathbf e_i\) gives \(w_i=z_i\).  The same support contains
 1346: \(2\mathbf e_i\), so its difference lattice is \(\mathbb Z^d\).  Finally,
 1347: the covariance matrix of \(\mathbf A\) is positive definite: if
 1348: \(\mathbf t\cdot\mathbf A\) were constant almost surely, comparison of
 1349: \(\mathbf e_i\) and \(2\mathbf e_i\) would give \(t_i=0\) for every
 1350: \(i\).
 1351: 
 1352: The fixed-denominator mean estimate quoted in the proof of
 1353: \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} gives
 1354: \[
 1355:  \sum_{\substack{1\le h<q\\(h,q)=1}}c(h/q)
 1356:  =O(q(1+\log q)^2).
 1357: \]
 1358: Since \(p_i z_i<1\), summation over exponent vectors proves
 1359: \(m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)<\infty\).
 1360: 
 1361: We now apply the smooth strictly minimal point expansion of
 1362: Pemantle--Wilson \cite{PemantleWilson2002}; the preceding paragraph verifies
 1363: its nonperiodicity, strict minimality, and nondegenerate transverse Hessian
 1364: for the present rational function.  Marking each letter by \(e^s\) and
 1365: using the expansion uniformly for small complex \(s\) gives the usual
 1366: analytic quasi-powers estimate.  The critical number of letters is determined
 1367: by
 1368: \[
 1369:  r_N\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}\mathbf A=N\boldsymbol\rho,
 1370:  \qquad\text{hence}\qquad r_N=\frac{N}{2\tau}.
 1371: \]
 1372: It follows that \(R_N/N\to1/(2\tau)\) in probability.
 1373: 
 1374: For \(M>0\), mark instead the bounded cost \(C\wedge M\).  The same analytic
 1375: perturbation gives
 1376: \begin{equation}
 1377:  \frac1N\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}(C_j\wedge M)
 1378:  \xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}(C\wedge M)}{2\tau}.
 1379:  \label{eq:prime-support-truncated-cost-law}
 1380: \end{equation}
 1381: To remove the truncation, put
 1382: \[
 1383:  D_M(\mathbf x):=
 1384:  \sum_{\text{letters }\ell}C(\ell)\mathbf1_{\{C(\ell)>M\}}
 1385:  \mathbf x^{\mathbf A(\ell)}.
 1386: \]
 1387: This series is analytic in a neighborhood of \(\mathbf z\).  Pointing one
 1388: letter in a free word gives the exact coefficient identity
 1389: \[
 1390:  \mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}C_j\mathbf1_{\{C_j>M\}}
 1391:  =\frac{[\mathbf x^{\mathbf n_N}]
 1392:   D_M(\mathbf x)(1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf x,1))^{-2}}
 1393:  {[\mathbf x^{\mathbf n_N}]
 1394:   (1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf x,1))^{-1}}.
 1395: \]
 1396: The corresponding smooth-point expansions, or equivalently differentiation
 1397: of the analytic perturbation
 1398: \(A_{\mathcal P}+sD_M\) at \(s=0\), yield
 1399: \[
 1400:  \frac1N\mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}
 1401:  C_j\mathbf1_{\{C_j>M\}}
 1402:  \longrightarrow
 1403:  \frac{\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}[C\mathbf1_{\{C>M\}}]}{2\tau}.
 1404: \]
 1405: First let \(N\to\infty\), then \(M\to\infty\), and use Markov's
 1406: inequality together with \eqref{eq:prime-support-truncated-cost-law}.  This
 1407: proves the cost limit in \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-free-law}; the
 1408: velocity limit follows from
 1409: \(N^{-1}\log q_{\mathbf n_N}\to\sum_i\rho_i\log p_i\).
 1410: 
 1411: Under \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-budget}, the free law gives
 1412: \[
 1413:  \frac{T_N}{m_N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}
 1414:  \frac{\alpha}{v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}}<1.
 1415: \]
 1416: Thus \(\Pr\{T_N\le m_N-1\}\to1\).  By
 1417: \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface}, the finite-window law on generator
 1418: words is obtained from the uniform free-word law by the weight
 1419: \(K_{m_N}(T_N)\).  Since this weight is bounded by four and equals four on
 1420: an event of probability tending to one, its normalizing constant tends to
 1421: four and the two laws converge in total variation.  This proves
 1422: \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-count} and
 1423: \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-window-law}.
 1424: 
 1425: On compact subsets of \(\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\), the saddle remains in a
 1426: compact subset of \(\prod_i(0,p_i^{-1})\), the transverse covariance stays
 1427: uniformly nondegenerate, and the fixed-denominator bound supplies a common
 1428: summable majorant for the cost tails.  The smooth-point expansions and all
 1429: truncation estimates above are therefore uniform there.
 1430: \end{proof}
 1431: \fi
 1432: 
 1433: \begin{remark}[Located active-cutoff obstruction]
 1434: \label{rem:prime-support-local-open-interface}
 1435: Equations \eqref{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal} and
 1436: \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface} locate, but do not solve, the
 1437: active arithmetic interface.  The unmarked rational function is in the scope
 1438: of smooth-point multivariate coefficient methods such as
 1439: Pemantle--Wilson \cite{PemantleWilson2002}; the nearest ordered-factorization
 1440: comparators are Lau \cite{Lau2001} and Hwang--Janson
 1441: \cite{HwangJanson2011}.  Those results do not retain the continued-fraction
 1442: cost at an exponent-conditioned lattice point with an error uniform in the
 1443: direction, and they do not provide the uniform integrability needed for the
 1444: unbounded cost.  Accordingly, no directional quenched law is asserted here,
 1445: even below the active cutoff.
 1446: 
 1447: Already for \(\mathcal P=\{2\}\), the normalized letter law used in the
 1448: dyadic renewal assigns mass \(3^{-a}\) to each fraction with denominator
 1449: \(2^a\).  Since
 1450: \(c(1/2^a)=2^{a+1}-1\), its cost \(C\) satisfies
 1451: \[
 1452:  \mathbb E C^2
 1453:  \ge\sum_{a\ge1}3^{-a}(2^{a+1}-1)^2=+\infty,
 1454:  \qquad
 1455:  \mathbb E e^{\theta C}=+\infty\quad(\theta>0).
 1456: \]
 1457: Its first moment is finite: the Panov--Liehl mean estimate used above makes
 1458: the contribution at exponent \(a\) at most
 1459: \(O(a^2(2/3)^a)\).  Thus neither a Gaussian local theorem nor a two-sided
 1460: Cram\'er tilt is available at \(u=1\).  A sharp asymptotic for
 1461: \(C_m(q_{\mathbf n})\) with the cutoff \(j=m+O(1)\) requires a lattice
 1462: stable or semistable local-renewal theorem uniform under multivariate exponent
 1463: conditioning, followed by the nonsmooth weights \(K_m(m)=3\) and
 1464: \(K_m(m+1)=1\).  No such theorem is proved or invoked here; this is the exact
 1465: remaining open input.
 1466: \end{remark}
 1467: 
 1468: \fi

 1470: \begin{theorem}[Finite-window conditional phase separation]

 1470: \begin{theorem}[Finite-window conditional phase separation]
 1471: \label{thm:conditional-phase-separation}
 1472: Fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\) and
 1473: \[
 1474:  0<\varepsilon<\min\{\alpha,v_c-\alpha\}.
 1475: \]
 1476: Let \(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}\) be uniform on
 1477: \[
 1478:  \left\{x\in X_m:
 1479:  \left|m^{-1}\log d_m(x)-\alpha\right|<\varepsilon\right\}.
 1480: \]
 1481: This set is nonempty for all sufficiently large \(m\), and
 1482: \begin{equation}
 1483:  \left(
 1484:   \frac1m\log d_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}),
 1485:   \frac1mL(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}))
 1486:  \right)
 1487:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}
 1488:  \left(\alpha+\varepsilon,
 1489:        \frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{v_c}\right).
 1490:  \label{eq:conditional-phase-separation}
 1491: \end{equation}
 1492: Consequently, for every \(\eta>0\),
 1493: \[
 1494:  \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\lim_{m\to\infty}
 1495:  \Pr\left\{
 1496:   \left|m^{-1}L(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}))
 1497:        -\frac\alpha{v_c}\right|>\eta
 1498:  \right\}=0.
 1499: \]
 1500: For fixed \(\varepsilon\), the outer limit is essential: if
 1501: \(0<\eta<\varepsilon/v_c\), then the probability in the last display tends
 1502: to one.
 1503: \end{theorem}
 1504: 
 1505: \begin{proof}
 1506: Put \(a=\alpha-\varepsilon\), \(b=\alpha+\varepsilon\), and
 1507: \(r=\mu_C/\mu_H=1/v_c\).  We first record the two renewal facts used below.
 1508: For each fixed \(h>0\), Blackwell's nonarithmetic renewal theorem
 1509: \cite[Ch.~XI]{Feller1971} gives
 1510: \begin{equation}
 1511:  \mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h\}\longrightarrow\frac h{\mu_H}.
 1512:  \label{eq:blackwell-critical-renewal}
 1513: \end{equation}
 1514: The law of \(H_1\) is nonarithmetic because it charges both \(\log2\) and
 1515: \(\log3\), whose ratio is irrational.  The strong law gives
 1516: \(S_n/T_n\to r\) almost surely.  Moreover, since \(H_i\ge\log2\), an interval
 1517: of length \(h\) contains at most
 1518: \(N_h:=\lfloor h/\log2\rfloor+1\) renewal epochs on every sample path.
 1519: Dominated convergence therefore yields, for each \(\delta>0\),
 1520: \begin{equation}
 1521:  \mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h,\ |S/T-r|>\delta\}\longrightarrow0.
 1522:  \label{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law}
 1523: \end{equation}
 1524: 
 1525: Choose \(\delta_0>0\) so that \((r+\delta_0)b<1\), and fix \(h>0\).
 1526: On
 1527: \[
 1528:  J_m=(bm-2h,bm-h]
 1529: \]
 1530: the part of \(\mathcal U\) satisfying \(|S/T-r|\le\delta_0\) has mass
 1531: \(h/\mu_H+o(1)\) by
 1532: \eqref{eq:blackwell-critical-renewal}--\eqref{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law}.
 1533: For all sufficiently large \(m\), every such point has \(S\le m-1\), and
 1534: hence \(K_m(S)=4\).  If \(Z_m\) denotes the cardinality of the conditioning
 1535: set, \eqref{main-eq:marked-microcanonical-count} gives
 1536: \begin{equation}
 1537:  Z_m\ge4e^{\sigma_0(bm-2h)}
 1538:        \left(\frac h{\mu_H}+o(1)\right).
 1539:  \label{eq:conditional-denominator-lower}
 1540: \end{equation}
 1541: 
 1542: For fixed \(\delta>0\), set
 1543: \[
 1544:  \rho_m:=\sup_{t\ge am-h}
 1545:  \mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h,\ |S/T-r|>\delta\}.
 1546: \]
 1547: Equation \eqref{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law} implies \(\rho_m\to0\).
 1548: Covering \((am,bm)\) from its upper endpoint by intervals of length \(h\),
 1549: and using \(K_m\le4\), bounds the number of exceptional states by
 1550: \[
 1551:  \frac{4e^{\sigma_0bm}}{1-e^{-\sigma_0h}}\rho_m.
 1552: \]
 1553: After division by \eqref{eq:conditional-denominator-lower}, this proves
 1554: \[
 1555:  \frac{L(g_m)}{\log d_m}=\frac ST\xrightarrow{\Pr}r
 1556: \]
 1557: under the conditional law.
 1558: 
 1559: Finally, \(\mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h\}\le N_h\) for every \(t\).  Thus, for
 1560: each \(\gamma>0\), the number of conditioned states with
 1561: \(T\le(b-\gamma)m\) is
 1562: \(O(me^{\sigma_0(b-\gamma)m})\).  Equation
 1563: \eqref{eq:conditional-denominator-lower} shows that their conditional
 1564: probability tends to zero.  Since the conditioning itself gives
 1565: \(T/m<b\), we have \(T/m\to b\) in probability.  Combining this with
 1566: \(S/T\to r\) proves \eqref{eq:conditional-phase-separation}; its stated
 1567: consequences follow immediately.
 1568: \end{proof}
 1569: 
 1570: \begin{corollary}[Admissible shrinking windows]
 1571: \label{cor:shrinking-window-phase-separation}
 1572: Under the hypotheses of \Cref{thm:conditional-phase-separation}, suppose that
 1573: \(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\) and
 1574: \(\liminf_{m\to\infty}m\varepsilon_m>0\).  Then the conditioning sets are
 1575: nonempty for all sufficiently large \(m\), and
 1576: \[
 1577:  \frac1mL(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon_m}))
 1578:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}\frac\alpha{v_c}.
 1579: \]
 1580: \end{corollary}
 1581: 
 1582: \begin{proof}
 1583: Choose a fixed \(h>0\) smaller than
 1584: \(\liminf m\varepsilon_m\).  The interval
 1585: \(((\alpha+\varepsilon_m)m-2h,
 1586:    (\alpha+\varepsilon_m)m-h]\) then lies inside the conditioning window for
 1587: all sufficiently large \(m\).  The proof of
 1588: \Cref{thm:conditional-phase-separation}, with this fixed interval, gives the
 1589: same denominator lower bound and the same uniform estimate
 1590: \(S/T\to1/v_c\).  The conditioning itself now gives \(T/m\to\alpha\), which
 1591: proves the assertion.
 1592: \end{proof}
 1593: 
 1594: \begin{remark}[Arithmetic obstruction for thinner windows]
 1595: \label{rem:shrinking-window-arithmetic-obstruction}
 1596: The preceding corollary cannot be extended to every sequence
 1597: \(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\).  Put
 1598: \(\mathcal D_m=\{\log d_m(x):x\in X_m\}\).  For every
 1599: \(0<\alpha<v_c\), there are infinitely many \(m\) for which
 1600: \(\alpha m\notin\mathcal D_m\).  Indeed, otherwise \(e^{\alpha m}\) and
 1601: \(e^{\alpha(m+1)}\) would be integers for all large \(m\), forcing
 1602: \(e^\alpha\) to be an integer; this contradicts
 1603: \(0<\alpha<v_c\le\alpha_+=\tfrac12\log\varphi<\log2\).
 1604: Enumerate such a subsequence as \((m_j)\).  Starting from any
 1605: \(\varepsilon_1>0\), define the remaining widths recursively so that
 1606: \(0<\varepsilon_m<\varepsilon_{m-1}/2\), and, when \(m=m_j\), impose also
 1607: \[
 1608:  \varepsilon_m<\frac1m
 1609:  \operatorname{dist}(\alpha m,\mathcal D_m).
 1610: \]
 1611: This gives a decreasing sequence for which the conditioning set is empty at
 1612: every \(m_j\).  Thus windows on a smaller scale require an additional
 1613: arithmetic local theorem, not merely a uniform version of Blackwell renewal.
 1614: \end{remark}
 1615: 
 1616: The preceding obstruction concerns arbitrary thin windows.  A complete
 1617: arithmetic conditional limit can nevertheless be obtained along the dyadic
 1618: multiplier ray.  For \(r\ge1\), let
 1619: \[
 1620:  \mathcal G_r:=\{g:\text{\(g\) is a Weinstein generator and }R(g)=2^r\}.
 1621: \]
 1622: 
 1623: \begin{theorem}[Exact dyadic multiplier limit]
 1624: \label{thm:dyadic-exact-multiplier}
 1625: The series
 1626: \begin{equation}
 1627:  \gamma_2:=\frac13\sum_{a\ge1}3^{-a}
 1628:  \sum_{\substack{1\le p<2^a\\p\ \mathrm{odd}}}c(p/2^a)
 1629:  \label{eq:dyadic-cost-velocity}
 1630: \end{equation}
 1631: converges.  Moreover,
 1632: \[
 1633:  \card{\mathcal G_r}=3^{r-1}\qquad(r\ge1).
 1634: \]
 1635: If \(G_r\) is uniform on \(\mathcal G_r\), then
 1636: \begin{equation}
 1637:  \frac{L(G_r)}r\xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{\Pr}\gamma_2.
 1638:  \label{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge}
 1639: \end{equation}
 1640: 
 1641: Let \(r_m\to\infty\) be integers such that
 1642: \[
 1643:  \frac m{r_m}\longrightarrow\beta>\gamma_2.
 1644: \]
 1645: Then
 1646: \begin{equation}
 1647:  C_m(2^{r_m})\sim4\cdot3^{r_m-1}.
 1648:  \label{eq:dyadic-finite-window-count}
 1649: \end{equation}
 1650: In particular, the exact level \(\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=2^{r_m}\}\) is nonempty
 1651: for all sufficiently large \(m\).  If \(\mathbf X_m^{(2)}\) is uniform on
 1652: this level, then
 1653: \begin{equation}
 1654:  \frac{L(g_m(\mathbf X_m^{(2)}))}{r_m}
 1655:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}\gamma_2,
 1656:  \qquad
 1657:  \frac{L(g_m(\mathbf X_m^{(2)}))}{m}
 1658:  \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}\frac{\gamma_2}{\beta}.
 1659:  \label{eq:dyadic-finite-window-bridge}
 1660: \end{equation}
 1661: Equivalently, with \(v_2=\log2/\gamma_2\), if
 1662: \(r_m=\lfloor\alpha m/\log2\rfloor\) and \(0<\alpha<v_2\), the last limit is
 1663: \(\alpha/v_2\).
 1664: \end{theorem}
 1665: 
 1666: \begin{proof}
 1667: If a free generator word has multiplier \(2^r\), every denominator in the
 1668: word is a power of \(2\).  For each \(a\ge1\), there are
 1669: \(\phi_{\rm E}(2^a)=2^{a-1}\) letters with denominator \(2^a\).  Hence the
 1670: ordinary generating function for such words, including the empty word, is
 1671: \[
 1672:  \frac1{1-\sum_{a\ge1}2^{a-1}z^a}
 1673:  =\frac{1-2z}{1-3z}.
 1674: \]
 1675: Coefficient extraction proves \(\card{\mathcal G_r}=3^{r-1}\) for \(r\ge1\).
 1676: 
 1677: Define an independent letter sequence \((A_i,C_i)_{i\ge1}\) by
 1678: \[
 1679:  \Pr\{(A_i,C_i)=(a,c(p/2^a))\}=3^{-a}
 1680:  \quad(a\ge1,\ 1\le p<2^a,\ p\ \mathrm{odd}).
 1681: \]
 1682: This is a probability distribution because
 1683: \[
 1684:  \sum_{a\ge1}2^{a-1}3^{-a}=1,
 1685:  \qquad \mathbb E A_1=3.
 1686: \]
 1687: For the canonical regular continued fraction of \(p/2^a\), the
 1688: Panov--Liehl fixed-denominator mean theorem
 1689: \cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} gives, as \(a\to\infty\),
 1690: \[
 1691:  2^{1-a}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<2^a\\p\ \mathrm{odd}}}
 1692:  c(p/2^a)=O(a^2).
 1693: \]
 1694: Consequently \(\mathbb E C_1<\infty\), and
 1695: \(\mathbb E C_1/\mathbb E A_1=\gamma_2\).
 1696: 
 1697: Put \(A^{(n)}=\sum_{i=1}^nA_i\) and
 1698: \(C^{(n)}=\sum_{i=1}^nC_i\).  Every word with total exponent \(r\) has
 1699: probability \(3^{-r}\).  Therefore
 1700: \[
 1701:  \Pr\{A^{(n)}=r\text{ for some }n\}
 1702:  =3^{-r}\card{\mathcal G_r}=\frac13.
 1703: \]
 1704: Conditioned on this renewal event, the word is uniform on \(\mathcal G_r\).
 1705: The strong law gives
 1706: \(C^{(n)}/A^{(n)}\to\mathbb E C_1/\mathbb E A_1=\gamma_2\) almost surely.
 1707: On every sample path, the indicator that a renewal visit to \(r\) violates a
 1708: fixed neighborhood of this limit therefore tends to zero as \(r\to\infty\).
 1709: Dominated convergence, followed by division by the constant hitting
 1710: probability \(1/3\), proves \eqref{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge}.
 1711: 
 1712: For \(j\ge0\), let
 1713: \[
 1714:  U_j(2^r):=\card{\{g\in\mathcal G_r:L(g)=j\}}.
 1715: \]
 1716: The four-branch formula \eqref{main-eq:marked-microcanonical-count} gives, for
 1717: \(r\ge2\),
 1718: \begin{equation}
 1719:  C_m(2^r)=\sum_{j\ge0}K_m(j)U_j(2^r).
 1720:  \label{eq:dyadic-four-branch-count}
 1721: \end{equation}
 1722: Under the hypotheses on \(r_m\),
 1723: \eqref{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge} implies
 1724: \[
 1725:  \Pr\{L(G_{r_m})\le m-1\}\longrightarrow1,
 1726: \]
 1727: because \(m/r_m\to\beta>\gamma_2\).  Since \(0\le K_m\le4\) and
 1728: \(K_m(j)=4\) for \(j\le m-1\), division of
 1729: \eqref{eq:dyadic-four-branch-count} by \(4\card{\mathcal G_{r_m}}\) proves
 1730: \eqref{eq:dyadic-finite-window-count}.  The cost law under the uniform exact
 1731: fiber level is obtained from the uniform law on \(\mathcal G_{r_m}\) by the
 1732: bounded weight \(K_m/4\), which converges to \(1\) in probability and in
 1733: \(L^1\).  Thus the two laws have total-variation distance tending to zero.
 1734: Equation \eqref{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge} now gives
 1735: \eqref{eq:dyadic-finite-window-bridge}.  The final formulation follows from
 1736: \(r_m/m\to\alpha/\log2\).
 1737: \end{proof}
 1738: 
 1739: \begin{remark}
 1740: The theorem identifies the quenched speed on one arithmetic multiplier ray.
 1741: The dyadic exponent--cost lattice has no proper sublattice obstruction:
 1742: the two letter vectors \((1,3)\) and \((2,7)\) have determinant \(1\).
 1743: The comparison with the critical annealed velocity requires the explicit
 1744: certificate given next.
 1745: \end{remark}
 1746: 
 1747: \begin{theorem}[Strict dyadic--critical speed separation]
 1748: \label{thm:strict-speed-separation}
 1749: The dyadic velocity in \Cref{thm:dyadic-exact-multiplier} and the critical
 1750: velocity in \Cref{main-thm:full-fiber-ldp} satisfy
 1751: \begin{equation}
 1752:  \begin{gathered}
 1753:   5.185556312796440793\le\gamma_2
 1754:   \le5.235362589164389880,\\
 1755:   v_2\ge0.132397168057576261,
 1756:  \end{gathered}
 1757:  \label{eq:dyadic-speed-certificate}
 1758: \end{equation}
 1759: and
 1760: \begin{equation}
 1761:  v_c\le0.131225207544812100.
 1762:  \label{eq:critical-speed-certificate}
 1763: \end{equation}
 1764: Consequently,
 1765: \begin{equation}
 1766:  v_2-v_c\ge0.001171960512764161>0.
 1767:  \label{eq:strict-speed-separation}
 1768: \end{equation}
 1769: 
 1770: In particular, fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\), put
 1771: \(r_m=\lfloor\alpha m/\log2\rfloor\), and condition uniformly on the exact
 1772: level \(d_m=2^{r_m}\).  This level is nonempty for all sufficiently large
 1773: \(m\), and
 1774: \begin{equation}
 1775:  \frac{L(g_m)}m\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}
 1776:  \frac{\alpha}{v_2}\ne\frac{\alpha}{v_c}.
 1777:  \label{eq:dyadic-uniform-speed-counterexample}
 1778: \end{equation}
 1779: Thus the critical conditional speed does not extend uniformly from bands to
 1780: all realizable exact multiplier sequences.
 1781: \end{theorem}
 1782: 
 1783: \begin{proof}
 1784: For a reduced fraction \(p/q\in(0,1)\), let \(S(p/q)\) denote the sum of the
 1785: partial quotients in its canonical regular continued fraction, so that
 1786: \(c(p/q)=2S(p/q)-1\).  Set
 1787: \[
 1788:  \begin{gathered}
 1789:   T(q):=\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}S(p/q),
 1790:   \qquad
 1791:   \mathcal C(q):=2T(q)-\phi_{\rm E}(q),\\
 1792:   A(q):=\sum_{n=1}^{q-1}\tau(n)\tau(q-n).
 1793:  \end{gathered}
 1794: \]
 1795: We first establish an explicit bound for \(\mathcal C(q)\).  Each of the
 1796: \(S(p/q)-1\) strict ancestor intervals of \(p/q\) in the Stern--Brocot tree
 1797: has ordered adjacent endpoints, say \(a/b<c/d\), and there are unique
 1798: positive integers \(u,v\) such that
 1799: \[
 1800:  \frac pq=\frac{ua+vc}{ub+vd}.
 1801: \]
 1802: Ordered coprime denominators \(b,d\) determine their Farey adjacent interval
 1803: uniquely.  Hence the map from a fraction together with one of its strict
 1804: ancestors to \((b,u,d,v)\) is injective, and
 1805: \begin{equation}
 1806:  T(q)-\phi_{\rm E}(q)\le A(q).
 1807:  \label{eq:ancestor-divisor-injection}
 1808: \end{equation}
 1809: Indeed, the quadruples on the right are counted by factoring
 1810: \(n=bu\) and \(q-n=dv\).
 1811: 
 1812: Use
 1813: \(\tau(n)\le2\sum_{e\mid n,\,e\le\sqrt n}1\), and let
 1814: \[
 1815:  N_q(e,f):=\card{\{1\le n<q:e\mid n,\ f\mid q-n\}}.
 1816: \]
 1817: If \(N_q(e,f)>0\), then \((e,f)\mid q\), and the Chinese remainder theorem
 1818: gives
 1819: \[
 1820:  N_q(e,f)\le\frac{q(e,f)}{ef}+1.
 1821: \]
 1822: Writing \(e=gh\), \(f=gk\), dropping the coprimality of \(h,k\), and using
 1823: the harmonic-sum bound gives
 1824: \[
 1825:  \sum_{\substack{e,f\le\sqrt q\\(e,f)\mid q}}
 1826:  \frac{(e,f)}{ef}
 1827:  \le\sum_{g\mid q}\frac1g
 1828:        \left(1+\frac12\log q\right)^2.
 1829: \]
 1830: For \(q=2^a\), the divisor sum is less than \(2\).  Since there are at most
 1831: \(q\) pairs \((e,f)\), it follows that
 1832: \[
 1833:  A(2^a)\le8\,2^a\left(1+\frac a2\log2\right)^2+4\,2^a.
 1834: \]
 1835: Combining this with \eqref{eq:ancestor-divisor-injection} and
 1836: \(\phi_{\rm E}(2^a)=2^{a-1}\) yields the all-range estimate
 1837: \begin{equation}
 1838:  \mathcal C(2^a)
 1839:  \le2^a\left\{\frac{17}{2}
 1840:        +16\left(1+\frac a2\log2\right)^2\right\}
 1841:  \qquad(a\ge1).
 1842:  \label{eq:dyadic-cost-sum-bound}
 1843: \end{equation}
 1844: The same argument for general \(q\), now using
 1845: \(\sum_{g\mid q}g^{-1}\le1+\log q\), also gives
 1846: \begin{equation}
 1847:  \mathcal C(q)\le q\{9+8(1+\log q)^3\}.
 1848:  \label{eq:general-cost-sum-bound}
 1849: \end{equation}
 1850: 
 1851: Exact Euclidean-algorithm enumeration through \(a=25\) gives
 1852: \[
 1853:  \gamma_2^{(25)}
 1854:  =\frac13\sum_{a=1}^{25}3^{-a}\mathcal C(2^a)
 1855:  =\frac{13180988392373}{2541865828329}.
 1856: \]
 1857: Using \(\log2<7/10\) in \eqref{eq:dyadic-cost-sum-bound}, the remaining
 1858: geometric quadratic sum is rational and satisfies
 1859: \[
 1860:  \begin{split}
 1861:  0\le\gamma_2-\gamma_2^{(25)}
 1862:  &\le\frac13\sum_{a=26}^{\infty}
 1863:   \left(\frac23\right)^a
 1864:   \left\{\frac{17}{2}+16\left(1+\frac{7a}{20}\right)^2\right\}\\
 1865:  &=\frac{126600871936}{2541865828329}.
 1866:  \end{split}
 1867: \]
 1868: Thus
 1869: \[
 1870:  \gamma_2\le\frac{4435863088103}{847288609443},
 1871: \]
 1872: which, with outward rounding of \(\log2\), proves
 1873: \eqref{eq:dyadic-speed-certificate}.
 1874: 
 1875: It remains to certify \(v_c\).  Put
 1876: \[
 1877:  R(s):=\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)},
 1878:  \qquad
 1879:  H:=-R'(\sigma_0),
 1880:  \qquad
 1881:  D:=\sum_{q\ge2}\mathcal C(q)q^{-\sigma_0}.
 1882: \]
 1883: Then \eqref{main-eq:critical-pressure-slopes} gives \(v_c=H/D\).  For completeness,
 1884: the interval computation uses, for \(s>1\),
 1885: \[
 1886:  \begin{split}
 1887:  Z_8(s)={}&\sum_{n=1}^{31}n^{-s}
 1888:  +\frac{32^{1-s}}{s-1}+\frac12 32^{-s}\\
 1889:  &+\sum_{k=1}^{8}\frac{B_{2k}}{(2k)!}
 1890:        (s)_{2k-1}32^{1-s-2k}.
 1891:  \end{split}
 1892: \]
 1893: The periodic-Bernoulli remainder and its differentiated form give
 1894: \begin{align*}
 1895:  |\zeta(s)-Z_8(s)|
 1896:  &\le\frac{|B_{16}|}{16!}(s)_{15}32^{-s-15},\\
 1897:  |\zeta'(s)-Z_8'(s)|
 1898:  &\le\frac{|B_{16}|}{16!}32^{-s-15}\\
 1899:  &\quad{}\times\left\{
 1900:   \frac{(s)_{16}'}{s+15}
 1901:   +(s)_{16}\left(\frac{\log32}{s+15}
 1902:                   +\frac1{(s+15)^2}\right)
 1903:  \right\}.
 1904: \end{align*}
 1905: Directed interval evaluation gives
 1906: \[
 1907:  \begin{split}
 1908:  R(2.4787)-2&\in
 1909:  [0.000131507893202250,0.000131507893202251],\\
 1910:  R(2.4788)-2&\in
 1911:  [-0.000127411416688770,-0.000127411416688769].
 1912:  \end{split}
 1913: \]
 1914: Since \(R\) is strictly decreasing,
 1915: \(\sigma_0\in[2.4787,2.4788]\).  Moreover,
 1916: \(-R'(s)=\sum_{q\ge2}\phi_{\rm E}(q)(\log q)q^{-s}\) is strictly decreasing.
 1917: The same interval calculation at the two endpoints gives the outward
 1918: certificate
 1919: \begin{equation}
 1920:  H\in[2.588435643856306896,2.589951928201414596].
 1921:  \label{eq:H-interval-certificate}
 1922: \end{equation}
 1923: 
 1924: Exact integer enumeration of \(\mathcal C(q)\) for \(2\le q\le20000\), with
 1925: only the powers \(q^{-s}\) evaluated by directed rounding, gives
 1926: \[
 1927:  \sum_{q=2}^{20000}\mathcal C(q)q^{-\sigma_0}
 1928:  \in[19.302273469878911856,19.311145544445741772].
 1929: \]
 1930: For the lower tail, fix \(1\le p\le7900\).  The elementary continued-fraction
 1931: identities for the symmetric residues imply, for \(q>20000\),
 1932: \[
 1933:  c(p/q)+c((q-p)/q)\ge\frac{4q}{p}-6.
 1934: \]
 1935: Indeed, \(p/q<1/2\), and replacing the initial partial quotient \(a_1\)
 1936: by \(1,a_1-1\) shows that \(S(p/q)=S((q-p)/q)\).  If \(p>1\), the first
 1937: Euclidean quotient is \(\lfloor q/p\rfloor\) and the remaining quotient sum
 1938: is at least \(2\); for \(p=1\), the quotient sum is \(q\).  These observations
 1939: give the displayed inequality (in fact, with room to spare).
 1940: The function
 1941: \(g_{p,s}(x)=4x^{1-s}/p-6x^{-s}\) is decreasing in the relevant range,
 1942: because
 1943: \[
 1944:  \frac{3(2.4787)(7900)}{2(2.4787-1)}<19864<20000.
 1945: \]
 1946: Every block of \(p\) consecutive integers contains exactly
 1947: \(\phi_{\rm E}(p)\) integers coprime to \(p\).  The selected residue pairs
 1948: are disjoint, and blockwise integration therefore gives
 1949: \[
 1950:  \begin{aligned}
 1951:  \sum_{q>20000}\mathcal C(q)q^{-\sigma_0}
 1952:  &\ge\sum_{p=1}^{7900}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(p)}p
 1953:  \bigg\{\frac{4(20000+p)^{2-\sigma_0}}
 1954:                  {p(\sigma_0-2)}\\
 1955:  &\hspace{43mm}
 1956:  -\frac{6(20000+p)^{1-\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0-1}\bigg\}\\
 1957:  &\ge0.434421763138508560.
 1958:  \end{aligned}
 1959: \]
 1960: The integrand is positive and decreases as \(s\) increases, so the final
 1961: outward evaluation is made at \(s=2.4788\).
 1962: For the opposite direction, \eqref{eq:general-cost-sum-bound} and the
 1963: integral test yield, with
 1964: \(f_s(x)=x^{1-s}\{9+8(1+\log x)^3\}\),
 1965: \[
 1966:  \begin{aligned}
 1967:  \sum_{q>20000}\mathcal C(q)q^{-\sigma_0}
 1968:  &\le f_{2.4787}(20000)
 1969:        +\int_{20000}^{\infty}f_{2.4787}(x)\,dx\\
 1970:  &\le347.686170804099215772.
 1971:  \end{aligned}
 1972: \]
 1973: Here direct differentiation shows that \(f_s\) is decreasing for
 1974: \(s\ge2.4787\) and \(x\ge20000\); the largest upper integrand occurs at
 1975: \(s=2.4787\).
 1976: Consequently,
 1977: \begin{equation}
 1978:  D\in[19.736695233017420416,366.997316348544957544].
 1979:  \label{eq:D-interval-certificate}
 1980: \end{equation}
 1981: Equations \eqref{eq:H-interval-certificate} and
 1982: \eqref{eq:D-interval-certificate} prove
 1983: \eqref{eq:critical-speed-certificate}; subtraction gives
 1984: \eqref{eq:strict-speed-separation}.  All integer enumerations, analytic
 1985: remainders, and rounding directions in this certificate are reproduced by
 1986: the accompanying script \texttt{verify\_speed\_separation.py}.
 1987: 
 1988: Finally, \(\alpha<v_c<v_2\) implies
 1989: \[
 1990:  \frac{m}{r_m}\longrightarrow\frac{\log2}{\alpha}>\gamma_2.
 1991: \]
 1992: The finite-window assertion of \Cref{thm:dyadic-exact-multiplier} applies and
 1993: gives \eqref{eq:dyadic-uniform-speed-counterexample}.
 1994: \end{proof}
 1995: 
 1996: \begin{proposition}[Polynomial conditional upper tail]
 1997: \label{prop:conditional-polynomial-upper-tail}
 1998: Under the fixed-window hypotheses of
 1999: \Cref{thm:conditional-phase-separation}, there exist \(\delta>0\) and
 2000: \(c>0\) such that, for all sufficiently large \(m\),
 2001: \[
 2002:  \Pr\left\{
 2003:   \frac1mL(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}))
 2004:   -\frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{v_c}>\delta
 2005:  \right\}
 2006:  \ge c m^{1-\sigma_0}.
 2007: \]
 2008: In particular, this upper deviation has exponential decay rate zero.
 2009: \end{proposition}
 2010: 
 2011: \begin{proof}
 2012: Retain \(b=\alpha+\varepsilon\) and \(r=1/v_c\), so that \(rb<1\).
 2013: Fix \(h>0\), and let \(Z_m\) denote the cardinality of the conditioning set.
 2014: Choose
 2015: \[
 2016:  0<\theta_0<\theta_1<\frac{1-rb}{2}
 2017: \]
 2018: and then \(\zeta>0\) sufficiently small that
 2019: \((r+\zeta)b+2\theta_1<1\) and
 2020: \(2\theta_0-\zeta b>0\).  For
 2021: \(\theta_0m\le q\le\theta_1m\), consider critical renewal words with
 2022: \[
 2023:  bm-\log q-2h<T\le bm-\log q-h,
 2024:  \qquad |S/T-r|\le\zeta.
 2025: \]
 2026: Equations \eqref{eq:blackwell-critical-renewal} and
 2027: \eqref{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law}, uniformly in this tail family of
 2028: intervals, show that their unweighted number is at least
 2029: \[
 2030:  c_0e^{\sigma_0bm}q^{-\sigma_0}
 2031: \]
 2032: for a fixed \(c_0>0\).  Append to each word the letter \(1/q\).  The free-word
 2033: decomposition makes all resulting words distinct, while
 2034: \[
 2035:  c(1/q)=2q-1,
 2036:  \qquad H(1/q)=\log q.
 2037: \]
 2038: Their total energies lie in \((bm-2h,bm-h]\).  By the choices of
 2039: \(\theta_1\) and \(\zeta\), their total costs are at most \(m-1\) for large
 2040: \(m\), so \(K_m=4\); by the choices of \(\theta_0\) and \(\zeta\), their
 2041: costs divided by \(m\) exceed \(rb\) by a fixed positive amount.
 2042: Consequently the exceptional-state count is at least
 2043: \[
 2044:  c_1e^{\sigma_0bm}
 2045:  \sum_{\theta_0m\le q\le\theta_1m}q^{-\sigma_0}
 2046:  \ge c_2e^{\sigma_0bm}m^{1-\sigma_0}.
 2047: \]
 2048: On the other hand, covering the whole conditioning interval by intervals of
 2049: length \(h\), using \(K_m\le4\) and the deterministic bound \(N_h\), gives
 2050: \(Z_m\le c_3e^{\sigma_0bm}\).  Division proves the asserted lower bound.
 2051: \end{proof}
 2052: 
 2053: \else
 2054: \begin{remark}[Imported and new components]
 2055: Weinstein's free-monoid parameterization, multiplicativity of \(R(g)\), and
 2056: largest-index formula \eqref{eq:generator-weight-length} are published inputs.
 2057: The weighted cost \(c(p/q)\), the finite-layer renewal identity
 2058: \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}, its transfer to the finite-window sums, and
 2059: the arithmetic and finite-size consequences of the pressure formula are the
 2060: additional conclusions here.  The pressure formula itself is the
 2061: golden-ratio Bernoulli-convolution \(L^q\)-spectrum in the normalization
 2062: \eqref{eq:erdos-pressure-dictionary}.  The orbit thinning and parity-complete
 2063: filling in
 2064: \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp} are the additional ingredients that close the
 2065: nonexposed large-deviation slopes.  The standard upper bound and the
 2066: exposed-slope tilting argument are not claimed as new.  The Stern--Brocot
 2067: multifractal theorem of Kesseb\"ohmer and Stratmann
 2068: \cite{KessebohmerStratmann2004,KessebohmerStratmann2007} concerns interval
 2069: growth rates and does not state the finite-window LDP above.  The
 2070: Stern--Brocot pressure and the Knauf
 2071: denominator-layer estimate
 2072: are cited published inputs.  In
 2073: particular, the argument does not reprove or invoke Weinstein's eventual
 2074: layer-stabilization theorem.  The marked identity
 2075: \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} is a direct bivariate restatement of
 2076: \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}, not an additional result.  The
 2077: arithmetic renewal theorem is classical; its endpoint finite-size transfer
 2078: and the uniform critical coexistence law in
 2079: \Cref{thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs} are additional finite-window
 2080: conclusions.
 2081: The separately compiled supplement records the conditional microcanonical,
 2082: dyadic-ray, and integer-moment consequences; they are not part of the main
 2083: theorem spine.
 2084: \end{remark}
 2085: \fi
